WHAT THE REPRICE MAKES IT, said plainly because it is the one thing a later reader will want: Charge now beats the free Strike on damage (25% against 23%), on Break (20 against 18) and on initiative (1.0 against 2.0), carries a Daze, and still NET-GENERATES 10 Rage (30 gained against its own 20 spent). Strike wins only on net Rage (+20) and on having no cooldown. It is therefore the first and only class-wide card in all twenty-four that generates its resource, and the only one that is not weaker than the free basic on every axis — a deliberate exception to BQ's rule rather than a drift past it. It also clears Overpower, a Warrior boss-pool card, on cost, damage, initiative and Rage economy, losing only on cooldown (3 against 1) and on Overpower's Break-scaling bonus. test_batch_br pins the exception BY NAME, asserts the two axes Strike still wins, and asserts that the other twenty-three class cards generate nothing, so a later batch cannot quietly add a second one.

WARCRY IS IMPLEMENTED AS +20% DAMAGE DEALT RATHER THAN +20% ATTACK, corrected toward the code and reported. §3 says "gains 20% Attack". attack is a raw stat read at dozens of sites — DoT snapshots, companion strikes, poison ticks — and a temporary mutation of it needs a revert path, which is the exact shape that produced this project's ~127,000 max-HP runaway (Batch W) and the reason the victory sync carries three separate fields. It rides Battle Shout's own read site, so there is one implementation of "this unit deals N% more damage", the two compose additively rather than one winning, and the number is identical where it is felt.

Machinery: what was reused, and the two new fields

  • RALLY REUSES THE EXISTING INITIATIVE HOOK. Ability.delay_push and the Cryomancer's Shattered Tempo both write next_time to push a unit along the timeline; _rally_forward is that same write aimed the other way, pulling the ally to just ahead of the earliest unit on the field. There is no second turn-order manipulator. It pulls to the FIELD minimum rather than to the caster's own clock, because the Warrior has already paid his delay by then and reading his clock would sometimes leave the ally behind an enemy that was already sooner — "acts next" has to mean next.
  • IT CANNOT PRODUCE UNBOUNDED CONSECUTIVE TURNS, and that is structural rather than hoped for. The ally spends the turn it is given and re-enters the order normally, the Warrior spent HIS to give it, the ability holds a 4-turn cooldown, and the caster is excluded from the target pool at three sites — the player's picker, the bot's pool, and _ability_usable (which refuses the cast outright when he is the last one standing). The special's own guard reads target != attacker as the belt to those braces.
  • BATTLE TRANCE READS DAMAGE TAKEN, NOT MISSING HEALTH, and that distinction IS the ability. BattleUnit.trance_taken is a dedicated accumulator written by _report_taken — the ONE door Batch BL built, so it books health actually removed, below every death refusal, and a future damage source cannot forget to report to it. It is cleared at every tick (and at the cast), which is what makes "since his last turn" true rather than "since the trance began". It is deliberately NOT a read of BL's dmg_by_turn: that is a fixed two-turn window keyed on the GLOBAL turn index, and this is the span between one hero's turns, which is however long the timeline makes it. _battle_trance_tick is its own function for the standing reason — _run_battle cannot be driven headlessly, so a rule left in its loop can only be checked by a grep and its negative control can never fail.
  • HUNTER'S MARK IS ONE MULTIPLIER WITH TWO CALLERS. _party_mark_mult is read by the strike loop AND by _companion_hit, because a beast is a separate damage path and the card says "the WHOLE party". Checked at the site rather than assumed from comp.is_hero, which is true of a companion and would have made "every beast reads it" look covered while it was not. The mark carries no owner index, which is the entire distinction from the Beastmaster's hunt_mark (that one stamps the hunter's index and pays him and his beast 25% plus Mana).
  • ARCANE ARROWS SPENDS ITS CHARGE AND DEALS ITS BLOW IN ONE FUNCTION, _arcane_arrow_splash, called from INSIDE the hit loop after the strike has resolved — so a MISSED or BLOCKED hit spends nothing, and an absolute parry that zeroed the damage spends nothing either. It reads final, the damage THIS hit actually dealt, rather than re-deriving it from the ability's nominal damage: the two would drift the moment a crit, a resist or an armor read differed. It does nothing and spends nothing when the struck enemy is the last one standing — a bank that drained itself against a single target would make the ability worst exactly where a Hunter fights most.
  • NO NEW UNIT FIELDS FOR TEN OF THE TWELVE. Everything with a duration is a status (camouflage, party_mark, battle_trance, warcry, ironclad), Arcane Arrows' charges live in their status's power BATTLE-LONG on the Mirror Image precedent, and Bola rides the EXISTING slow and cripple (authoring a third affliction would be the Choking Smoke mistake). ONE new field: trance_taken.

Verified

Per the designer's standing instruction, that means the code landed and works. NO SWEEPS, NO BANDS, NO BALANCE MEASUREMENT WAS RUN, and none should be quoted from this batch.

check_parse 0 · check_flow 0 · check_map_screen OK · run-harness gates 1/2/3 PASS · NEW test_batch_br.gd 1441/0 (1414 at ship; the Charge reprice added 27 checks — the exception by name, the two axes Strike still wins, and a sweep proving the other twenty-three class cards still generate nothing).

All six clauses §6 named as able to silently do nothing or do the wrong thing are driven live and asserted against the state they change, never against a cast returning — and six are built so a broken implementation still FAILS: the volley's charge spend is an EXACT identity (5 → 2) with a single-strike ability asserted to spend exactly one in the same check, which is what tells "three per volley" from "three per cast"; the charges are TICKED six times by hand before the count is re-read; Battle Trance is measured where the two readings differ by construction (140 missing health against 20 taken, so the right answer is 16 and the missing-health reading would be 76); Rally is asserted to make the ally the unit _next_unit actually returns rather than merely to have written a smaller number; Camouflage's combined chance is asserted HIGHER than either alone and LOWER than their sum, which only independent combination gives; and Iron Will's meter is asserted at EXACTLY 99, which both wrong implementations fail.

ELEVEN NEGATIVE CONTROLS, each applied to product code and reverted (battle.gd, unit.gd and classes.gd each came back byte-identical by hash, and the suite to 1414/0 after the last): Iron Will as "the meter cannot fill" trips 5; Iron Will as "Broken is refused" trips 3; Arcane Arrows spending one charge per CAST trips 3; Arcane Arrows put on a clock trips 5; Battle Trance reading MISSING HEALTH trips 2; Battle Trance's accumulator never cleared trips 2; Camouflage OVERWRITING Ghillie Suit trips 2; Hunter's Mark paying only the hero who marked trips 2; Mirror Image back to one image per CAST trips 2; a class ability leaking into CLASS_POOLS trips 2; Rally's ally pool no longer excluding the caster trips 1.

FULL BATTERY GREEN: ah 5500, ah_battle 65, ai 2217, aj 418, ak 528, al 560, ar 914, as 396, at 470, au 336, av 324, aw 350, ax 338, ay 484, az 519, ba 690, bb 172, bc 91, bd 69, be 34, bf 78, bg 47, bh 233, bi 88, bj 67, bl 88, bm 1890, bn 77, bo 505, bp 268, bq 738, br 1441, runes 2973, rune_battle 96 — all 0 failures. an reads 3624 and bk 130, both inside their DOCUMENTED run-to-run drift. The three raised counts are POOL LOOPS WALKING MORE ENTRIES rather than new assertions (each suite iterates the live pools, and twelve arrived; bq's jump is the largest because its "no class card is also in a spec pool" check is 12 names × 12 specs).

FIVE SUITES RE-POINTED IN PLACE with the reason in each file, and FOUR OF THE RE-POINTS ARE INVERSIONS — the honest treatment when a batch pays a debt an older suite was recording. test_batch_bo's, test_batch_bp's and test_batch_bq's "the Hunter and Warrior class pools are still EMPTY and still owed" became "all four are FILLED at six"; bo's and bq's "a Hunter's offer fills SHORT at two" became "it fills THREE", with the fill-short rule kept by wearing a pool down with the no-return ledger instead — that rule was never about which pool is thin, it is about an offer never padding with repeats. The setups are byte-identical, because they are still what tells the two answers apart. test_batch_ba's Ghillie Suit grep was re-pointed and it was a REAL CATCH (the Batch AZ Follow-Through precedent exactly): it looks for the literal 0.01 * target.ghillie to prove the node reads its own counter as the chance, and extracting the roll into _evade_chance deleted that exact text. The question is unchanged; the fragment is 0.01 * u.ghillie now.

TWO PRE-EXISTING FLAKES IN test_batch_bq FOUND AND CLOSED BY FORCED DETERMINISM, not retried (the AK/AL/AR discipline): its Unburden mitigation check and its Exhortation damage check each compare ONE blow against ONE blow with the crit roll live, and a 20% cut or a 25% buff cannot survive the other side landing a ×1.5 crit — each failed about one run in five against working code. Variance alone could never flip either (the ratio would have to exceed 1.25 and randf_range tops out at 1.1/0.9), which is what identifies the crit as the cause. KNOWN-BAD AND UNCHANGED: test_batch_ah and test_batch_an still call Run.award_talent_points, which Batch BM deleted, and both still print 0 failures while aborting their own sections; test_runes still prints its stale start_rune_enabled error; and test_batch_al's standing Spite flake reproduced once and passed on a clean re-run.

THE DESIGNER HAD NO RUN IN FLIGHT and none was created — no run_save.bin exists; relics.json and trees.json are byte-identical by hash after the battery and after a --run 6 walked ONLY to exercise RunSim's draft path (9.00 offers/run, 27.00 cards shown, taken 9.00 — three cards an offer, never short, which is the seam closing measured end to end). Its Matrix row is NOT a difficulty reading and none is quoted.

Live autoplay clean across real battles, 0 SCRIPT ERROR, ALL TWELVE FIRING IN ORDINARY FIGHTS — "Battle Trance: Berserker comes back 16 — 3% of maximum plus half of the 22 he took since his last turn" (the number that tells a live read apart from a low-health heal), "Berserker: Rally — Devout acts NEXT, straight to the front of the order", "Warcry — 4 heroes deal 20% more damage for 3 turns", "Field Dressing — heals 32 (24% of maximum) and shakes off Poison", and the §1 rule visible in three consecutive lines: "Aimed Volley … the shot forks into Orc Chief (5 charges left)" … "(4 charges left)" … "(3 charges left)", with a MISSED shot spending none. NOTE the smoke's own artefact: DOD_SIM_ABILITIES applies its list to EVERY hero, so a Cryomancer casts Charge in the log — the real draft only ever offers class-matching cards.

2026-08-13 — Batch BQ: the Mage and Cleric class pools

Twelve class-wide abilities, six per class, filling half the seam Batch BO opened. One draft card in four is class-wide, and until now that seam rolled into an empty pool for every hero in the game — a quarter of every offer was dead. The Hunter and Warrior class pools are NOT in this batch and are still owed: twelve of a target twenty-four ship here, and a Hunter's or a Warrior's offer still loses its class card. Nothing else ships — no talent node, no magnitude, no existing ability changed, and no save version moves (still v10).

A separate pool, for Batch BO's own reason

CLASS_POOLS is byte-untouched, and that is the negative control that matters. BO built SPEC_DRAFT_POOLS as a new structure rather than adding to SPEC_POOLS, because sharing one pool would have silently re-weighted every boss offer in the game the moment new entries landed. The identical argument applies here: CLASS_POOLS feeds boss picks today, so dropping six abilities into it would re-weight those offers as a side effect of a draft change. So the twelve live in CLASS_DRAFT_POOLS, keyed by class, and test_batch_bq asserts all four of CLASS_POOLS' arrays as literals rather than by size — a swap of two names would keep the count and change every draw. The tidy-looking edit a later batch would make — folding two class pools into one — is the thing that comment exists to refuse.

Drafted class abilities land in bm_abilities exactly as spec draft abilities do, so battle spawn, the hero sheet, the rune filter and upgrade pairing all pick them up with no new plumbing.

The Mage six — the tools every spine wants and no spine provides

Three spines — commitment, control, escalation — that are all fragile early and all mana-hungry, because a spine that provided its own answer would stop being a spine. The Pyromancer has no defence at all by design (Batch AR removed every option deliberately), the Arcanist's went to the vault with Stabilize (AT), and the Cryomancer's is a hold a boss shrugs.

  • Magic Barrier — 25 Mana, 2.0 init, 4cd, self. Absorbs 15% of maximum health for 3 turns (Perfect: 20%). The floor beneath all three. It reuses the existing barrier pipeline rather than authoring a second one, and it is deliberately not a Divine Shield: Faith is the Devout's engine, and a Mage's ward feeding Conviction would be a spec mechanic leaking out through a class card.
  • Mirror Image — 20 Mana, 2.0, 4cd, self. The next three single-target attacks against him miss (Perfect: four). Evasion rather than absorption. Better than the Barrier against three big hits, worse against a swarm or an AoE — the two are not a strict upgrade of each other in either direction, which is the class-pool rule applied inside one pool.
  • Magic Missiles — 15 Mana, 2.0, 2cd, one enemy. Three bolts at 12% of Attack each (Perfect: a fourth). The reliable filler. Multi-hit, so it plays with anything reading hits rather than casts.
  • Mana Well — 20 Mana, 1.5, 5cd, self. For 3 turns (Perfect: 4) Mana regeneration is doubled. Costs mana to make mana — net-positive only if the fight lasts.
  • Dispel — 15 Mana, 1.5, 3cd. Two harmful effects off an ally, or two beneficial effects off an enemy (Perfect: three). Utility nobody has, and the only ability in the game whose target may be either side.
  • Blink — 10 Mana, 1.0, 3cd, self. Clears one turn from every one of his cooldowns (Perfect: two); its own is exempt. Tempo, not damage — it scales with kit size rather than with any spec, which is the cleanest statement of "class-wide" in the pool.

The Cleric six — three spines with enormous conditional power and no baseline

None of the three can simply heal someone on turn one: Holy's healing is gated behind Mercy, the Devout's behind shields absorbing hits, the Occultist's behind marks on enemies. The names are chosen for register — the Occultist is a Cleric, and "Sanctified Ground" on him reads as a joke. These are priestly and old: rites, offices, practices — words about what a cleric does rather than which side he is on.

  • Ministration — 20 Mana, 2.0, 2cd, one ally. Heals 20% of their maximum health (Perfect: 26%). The plain heal none of them has.
  • Consecration — 25 Mana, 2.5, 5cd. For 3 turns (Perfect: 4) the whole party regenerates 5% of maximum health each turn. Sustained rather than burst — the opposite read to Ministration.
  • Chastise — 15 Mana, 2.0, 2cd, one enemy. 25% of Attack and 20 Break damage (Perfect: 30 BD). Something to do with a turn. See the finding below.
  • Unburden — 20 Mana, 1.5, 4cd, one ally. Removes every harmful effect, and they take 20% less damage for 2 turns (Perfect: 3). Cleanse with a tail — a pure cleanse is dead against warbands that apply nothing, and the mitigation half is what makes it never a wasted card.
  • Exhortation — 25 Mana, 2.5, 4cd. The whole party's next attack deals 25% more damage (Perfect: 35%). The only offensive party buff a Cleric has. Banked, not timed — it waits for each hero's next swing rather than expiring on a turn count, so a slow hero is not cheated of it.
  • Undying Vigil — 25 Mana, 2.0, 4cd, one ally. For 3 turns (Perfect: 4), whenever that ally is healed, a second ally on lower health is healed for half as much. Making one heal into two. It fits all three spines without duplicating any: Holy's big heals fork, the Devout's shield-conversion trickles outward, the Occultist's lifesteal spreads. It is the one class ability that gets better the more spec-specific the build is, a deliberate inversion of the unconditional-floor role the other five play.

No death-save clause, deliberately. Holy already has three (Resurrection, Intercession, Rite of Return) and a fourth in the class pool would make her the only spec whose class card duplicates her own identity. Name note: Undying Vigil sits near the Berserker's Undying Rage (Fury capstone) and near Holy's VIGIL lane — different objects, no mechanical collision, but three uses of two words, recorded so it is a known overlap rather than a discovered one.

The "weaker" half, verified rather than trusted — and the one card that fails it

Every comparison was checked against the live spec kits, with the arithmetic:

  • Ministration against Holy's Heal. Heal is 40% of the Cleric's maximum (150 → 60); Ministration is 20% of the target's, which tops out at 40 on the Warden's 200. The same Mercy multiplier scales both, so the gap survives every build. Lesser.
  • Magic Missiles against the Mage fillers. 3 × 12% = 36% against Razor Ice's 3 × 15% = 45% and Arcane Barrage's 6 × 8% = 48%, and 3 Break a bolt against Razor Ice's 10. Lesser.
  • Magic Barrier against Divine Shield. 15% of maximum against 30%, for more Mana and double the cooldown. Lesser.

THE FINDING, REPORTED AND NOT RE-TUNED: CHASTISE FAILS THE RULE IN THE OTHER DIRECTION — it is too weak to pick rather than too strong. The brief names both its numbers, and against them the free core attack wins on damage for all three Cleric specs: Smite is 44% of Attack with 16 BD at no cost and no cooldown; Shadowrend is 25% with 16 BD and a Cripple. Chastise pays 15 Mana and a 2-turn cooldown for 25% of Attack and 4 more Break, so it is dominated — there is no board state on which casting it beats simply attacking. It ships as specified, and test_batch_bq pins the comparison so a later reprice has to read the reasoning first. The lever is one of its two numbers and it is the designer's, not a batch's.

Break damage was assigned deliberately rather than by omission

Batch BO's own correction, applied up front. The brief names only Chastise's 20. Chastise 20 (named); Magic Missiles 3 per bolt — 9 across three, decided against its siblings: below Razor Ice's 10 a shard and level with Arcane Barrage's 3 while throwing half as many. The other ten carry none, because they are not attacks and Break from an ability that never strikes is Break from nowhere.

What was measured rather than assumed

  • Dispel's enemy half is thin, and §3 asked. shielded is the only beneficial status an enemy can carry in the whole game, applied by two of nineteen enemy kinds (the Orc Shieldmaster's Shielding and the Hollow Crown's Regalia). So that half strips at most one thing and usually nothing. Reported rather than dropped: the ally half is a real answer on its own, and authoring enemy buffs is a content decision that belongs to whoever wants one.
  • The exclusion list is the load-bearing half of Dispel. What may be stripped is derived (anything that is not a debuff), so an enemy buff a later batch authors is dispellable the day it lands. But DEBUFF_IDS deliberately does not hold the five marks the party applies — covenant, quarry, snare_line, feinted, hunt_mark — and left to the derived rule they read as "beneficial effects on an enemy", so Dispel would have stripped the party's own work. ruin_primed is the same trap through another door (it is the primer, not the mark, so it is not a debuff either, and dispelling it would defuse the Occultist's bomb for him), and charging is a declared blow rather than a boon — cancelling a wind-up is what a Break is for, and handing that to a 15-Mana utility card would quietly delete the Ash Hurler's whole lesson.
  • Mana Well doubles the live figure. Measured in a real fight at 44 a turn for a Mage — 12 base plus Evocation's 10, doubled — rather than a doubled hardcoded 12. The doubling lives in _mana_regen, the one place the drip's size is decided.

Two decisions and one extraction the brief did not name

  • Dispel targets either side through a widened picker pool, not a third Ability.Target value. An enum entry would have to be understood by the enemy AI's re-validation, the intent classifier, the bot's targeting and _ability_usable — four places needing a rule for a case one card uses. _resolve_special reads target.is_hero and knows which half it is; that is the whole cost.
  • The cooldown hook §3 said to reuse did not exist as a hook — it existed as four copies. Follow-Through, Practised Hands, Frostbound Hours and Crusader's Tempo each carried their own hand-written six lines. They all call _tick_cooldowns now, and they behave identically: cutting an entry already at zero was always a no-op, so folding the guard in changes nothing any of them did. Blink is the fifth caller. This project keeps re-learning what a number written twice costs (Batch BP's Eye of the Storm, where the chip silently won).
  • Mirror Image's images and Exhortation's share live in their status's power, battle-long, on the Interpose precedent — no new unit fields at all for twelve abilities. Battle-long is what makes banked, not timed true, and one place holding the number is what stops the chip and the arithmetic disagreeing.
  • No Cover bypasses Mirror Image and spends no image. That field is documented at _miss_chance as an absolute — "these attacks cannot be made to miss by any current or future source" — and Mirror Image is a future source. It is unreachable in play (only the Sharpshooter carries it and heroes do not attack the Mage), but the rule is honoured rather than quietly excepted. A later suite that arms no_cover on every unit for determinism, as test_batch_bp does, will see every image look broken; test_batch_bq arms it on the heroes only, and says why.

Verified

Per the standing instruction, that means the code landed and works. No sweeps, no bands, no balance measurement was run, and none should be quoted from this batch.

check_parse 0 · check_flow 0 · run-harness gates 1/2/3 PASS · new test_batch_bq.gd. All five clauses §6 named as able to silently do nothing are driven live and asserted against the state they change, never against a cast returning — and five are built so a broken implementation still fails: an area attack sits in the middle of Mirror Image's count so "an AoE spends none" cannot pass on charges that never move; Mana Well is measured on a Mage carrying Evocation's +10, where the honest answer (44) and the stale-constant answer (24) differ; a party mark is put on the same enemy Dispel is pointed at; Blink's cast asserts a drafted ability's cooldown and its own in opposite directions; and Undying Vigil's board carries an ally above the warded one and an ally between, so "heals whoever is lowest" and "re-heals the warded one" both fail.

Nine negative controls, each applied to product code and reverted (both touched files came back byte-identical by hash): an image spent by an area attack trips 3; Mana Well doubling a hardcoded 12 trips 1; Dispel's exclusion list removed trips 3; Blink refunding its own cooldown trips 1; the vigil fork's re-entrancy guard deleted trips 1; Exhortation put on a clock trips 3; a class-wide ability leaking into CLASS_POOLS trips 2; Ministration reading the caster's maximum trips 1; the Exhortation spend site removed trips 2.

Live autoplay clean across three real battles, 0 SCRIPT ERROR, with all twelve firing in ordinary fights — including "Mana Well — regeneration doubled to 44 a turn", which is the number that tells a live read apart from a doubled constant. Full battery green; test_batch_az's Follow-Through source-grep was re-pointed at _tick_cooldowns (the extraction deleted the literal it looked for, and the question it asks is unchanged), and six other suites were re-pointed in place with the reason in each file, four of them inversions of assertions that recorded the debt this batch paid.

2026-08-13 — Batch BP: the Warrior draft pools

Six abilities, two per Warrior spec, closing the debt Batch BO left open. All three Warrior draft pools were named and empty, so a Warrior's draft offer filled short in every run — one of four heroes in every party had no draft at all. Nothing else ships: no talent node, no magnitude, no existing ability changed, and no save version moves (still v10). The 24 class-wide abilities are still owed and are not in this batch.

The arriving-stance principle, first, because it is the standing rule the batch leaves behind

The Swordmaster's stances were a binary toggle with passive numbers on each side, and nothing in his kit had ever behaved differently depending on which one he was in. Both of his new cards read the stance and then switch it — and the thing that makes the switch a feature rather than a tax is the rule they are both authored against:

Each branch buys what the stance he is ARRIVING in wants. Cast from Aggressive he lands in Defensive, so the ability hands him defence. Cast from Defensive he lands in Aggressive, so it hands him offence. He is never stranded — he always arrives holding something. This is now a standing design rule, recorded because it is the thing a later stance ability would most easily get backwards. Stance-GATED abilities — ones that require a stance to cast rather than branching on it — are a noted future direction and were deliberately not built here.

The pivot itself is one implementation with three callers now (_swordmaster_switch): Guard Change, Precision Strike and Feint. Tempo is part of the pivot rather than part of Guard Change, and that is a decision rather than a convenience — the node's own text reads "switching stance grants +30% damage for 1 turn" and names no ability, so a swap that skipped it would make a shipped tooltip false. Everything Guard Change pays beyond the pivot — its Break damage, Sunder Guard, No Quarter, the parry perfect — stays on Guard Change, because those are on its card and no other.

The six

Berserker — how do you get low, and what do you do with the blood. His damage scales with missing health and his Bloodletting lane is built entirely on bleedout events — and he controlled neither. Enemy damage decided when he reached his power band; a 100-point buildup decided when four of his own nodes fired.

  • Blood Offering — 0 Rage, 1.5 initiative, 3cd, self. He loses 20% of his CURRENT health and gains 40 Rage (Perfect: 60). Axis: buying the frenzy band on purpose. Percent of current, not maximum, is the whole safety argument: it can never take him below 1, and its absolute cost shrinks as he drops, which is correct for a spec that wants to live low rather than die low. The health is removed directly rather than through the damage path — this is a price he pays, not a wound anyone dealt, and routing it through take_hit would have fed Blood Price, the recap's damage-taken ledger and every on-damage rider with an event that had no attacker.
  • Gut Rip — 30 Rage, 2.5, 4cd, one enemy. Bleeds the target out at once whatever its buildup, and deals 6% of Attack for every 10 points consumed (Perfect: 9%). Axis: the bleedout stops being the enemy's clock. It fires the REAL bleedout path — the meter is topped up to 100 through _add_bleed_with_burst rather than a private copy of the burst being written into the ability — so Bloodcraze, Scent of Blood, Arterial Spray and Blood Tithe all answer it, the Exsanguination capstone and the Rune of Exsanguination still apply, and Slaughterhouse still leaves the meter at 50 rather than 0, which means a second Gut Rip four turns later has half a wound waiting for it. A private burst would have been the one thing that made the ability do nothing for the lane it exists to serve.

Swordmaster — what is a stance worth.

  • Precision Strike — 20 Rage, 2.0, 3cd, one enemy, then switches stance. From Aggressive: strikes twice for 20% of Attack each and grants +25% parry chance for 3 turns. From Defensive: strikes once for 15% of Attack with 15 Break damage, and his attacks bypass all armor for 3 turns. Perfect: the stance it grants holds 4 turns. Axis: the same blade, two intentions. Armor is bypassed outright rather than penetrated by a percentage — armor penetration in this game is a fraction of the target's armor, so a percentage clause would have been "+50% of zero" against every unarmoured enemy in the game: a clause that silently does nothing, which is the exact dud the upgrade pool's eligibility rule exists to prevent. It reuses the existing bypass hook that Held Breath and Through and Through already ride.
  • Feint — 25 Rage, 2.0, 4cd, one enemy, then switches stance. From Aggressive: strikes for 35% of Attack, and the enemy's next attack is redirected at one of its own allies. From Defensive: no strike at all — he gains 2 charges (Perfect: 3), each of which parries an attack against him outright and deals its damage to the attacker instead. Axis: their swing lands somewhere they did not intend. Charges, not turns: both halves wait until spent, so a Feint cast into a lull is not wasted and cannot be dodged by an enemy simply not attacking that turn.

Warden — what does protection look like when it is not his own. Block is his signature stat and it had only ever protected him; Taunt brought damage to him one enemy at a time.

  • Covering Guard — 25 Rage, 2.5, 4cd, one ally. For 3 turns (Perfect: 4), his Block chance is rolled against attacks targeting that ally, and a success negates the attack entirely. Axis: his stat protecting someone else. This is not redirection — nothing moves to him. The attack simply stops, which is what Block does and what nothing else in the game does. It reads his live Block chance, so Shieldwall, Heavy Plating's climb and Bulwark Line all feed a ward laid turns earlier; a snapshot at cast time would have read exactly like the ability working while being quietly worse than the card. It rides its own slice of the existing block roll with its own label, so Tenacity and Rally (which test for "Heavy Plating") never fire off a covered ally's block — the same treatment Bulwark Line already has. Cast on another ally only, and that is structural rather than a warning: the picker's pool, the bot's pool and the usable gate are all narrowed.
  • Eye of the Storm — 20 Rage, 2.0, 4cd, self. Taunts every enemy for 2 turns (Perfect: 3), and he takes 8% less damage per enemy taunted. Axis: being outnumbered becomes the point. A party-wide defensive cooldown that costs him everything, and it is self-balancing — a bigger field means more mitigation as well as more incoming. The mitigation reads the number actually taunted rather than the number alive, and it is bounded by the six-body field cap rather than by a ceiling of its own. Flagged: this may be too strong. Two turns where the party takes nothing is a large effect; the mitigation scaling keeps him alive against a wide field but not necessarily against a small one with heavy hitters, which is roughly the right tension. It ships untuned and is to be watched in play.

Break damage, assigned deliberately rather than by omission

Batch BO caught that this batch's predecessor brief specified cost, initiative, cooldown, target and effect and said nothing about pressure — the same omission that turned Death Ray's absent Break into a three-batch thread. Only Precision Strike's Defensive branch names a figure (15). The rest were decided against their siblings and are reported here:

  • Precision Strike, Aggressive: 6 per strike (12 across two) — deliberately below the Defensive branch's 15. The Defensive branch is the one whose card names Break as its own clause; the Aggressive branch pays in damage and parry instead, and giving it the better Break total would have undermined the split.
  • Feint's Aggressive strike: 12 — below both, and well below Overpower's 20 and Pommel Strike's 30 on the same hero. It is a trick rather than a hammer; its payoff is the redirect.
  • Gut Rip: 20, in line with Bloodlust's 18 and Crushing Blow's 20.
  • Blood Offering, Covering Guard and Eye of the Storm: none. They are not attacks, and Break arriving from an ability that never strikes would be Break from nowhere.

Two adjacencies, flagged rather than discovered — and a third the batch found

  • Feint's redirect is adjacent to the Occultist's Madness lane, and it is distinguished structurally rather than by assertion: it takes ONE named attack and nothing else — no status on the victim, no Daze, no Ruin, no persistence, and the enemy still chose its own action and still spends its own turn. Bewitch, Psychosis and Hysteria take the turn, and are counted as hijacked declarations for exactly that reason; the redirect honours the declaration, so it is deliberately not counted as one. The fallback the brief offered — a straight miss instead of a redirect — was not needed and was not taken.
  • Feint's Defensive charges are adjacent to Riposte (answer every parry with a free Overpower). They resolve cleanly together: a Feint charge is a parry, so Riposte answers it once, and the reflect fires once. Nothing double-counts.
  • The closer neighbour is WAITING GUARD (Poise row 8), which banks up to three guaranteed parries — and the batch found it rather than the brief naming it. The two are distinguished by the reflect, which only Feint's charges carry, and by the ORDER in which they spend: the renewable bank goes first. Waiting Guard re-banks itself every turn he goes undamaged while a Feint charge costs 25 Rage and a turn, so spending the free one first preserves the paid one — the same argument that already put Waiting Guard before the parry roll, extended one step.

Corrections toward the code

  • "Guard Change is the only stance swap in the game" is no longer true, and the protected-core table says so. It is still the Swordmaster's enabler, for a sharper reason than before: it is the only unconditional swap. The other two are drafted (he may never be offered either), cost Rage, and sit on 3- and 4-turn cooldowns, so a passive that is half inert without a swap still cannot be left depending on them.
  • Feint's mark is deliberately NOT a debuff, joining the covenant, quarry and snare-line absences for the same reason and one more. It is a mark — a misdirection the Swordmaster committed, not an affliction on the body — and it waits until spent, i.e. battle-long, which the cleanse helper reads as 999 turns remaining. A mender's longest-first pick would therefore have taken it every single time, which is the exact fault the Glacial Hold was carved out for.

One name collision, reported not acted on

PRECISION STRIKE (the ability) sits one letter from PRECISION STRIKES, a Swordmaster talent node (Blade row 4, bonus crit against Dazed/Crippled/Exposed targets) — and unlike the Second Wind collision Batch BO shipped, these two are in the SAME spec, so a player building a Swordmaster meets both. It is a LABEL collision only: a talent node's name is not an ability name, nothing resolves it, the fields are separate (precision_ranks against the precision_strike special), and nothing breaks. Shipped as specified and flagged, on the Second Wind / Shared Vigil / Overkill precedent — renaming either is the designer's call and one string.

Verified

Per the standing instruction from Batch BO onward, verified means the code landed and works. No sweeps, no bands, no balance measurement was run, and none should be quoted from this batch.

check_parse 0 · check_flow 0 (11 screens) · check_map_screen OK · run-harness gates 1/2/3 PASS · new test_batch_bp.gd 260/0 · full battery green. All five clauses the batch named as able to silently do nothing are driven live and asserted against the state they change, never against a cast returning.

Ten negative controls, each applied to product code and reverted (the file came back byte-identical by hash): Blood Offering reading MAXIMUM health trips 3; Gut Rip writing a private burst instead of the real bleedout trips 3; the arriving-stance principle inverted trips 4; Covering Guard snapshotting the Block chance at cast time trips 1; Feint's charges expiring on a clock trips 4; Eye of the Storm paying a flat rate trips 1; a Feint charge no longer being an absolute parry trips 1; the redirect never firing trips 3; Covering Guard's ally pool no longer excluding the Warden trips 1; and a self-ward becoming a second slice of his own roll trips 1.

One of them found a fault in this batch's own code, which is the entire reason to run them. Eye of the Storm's mitigation was written three times in six lines — the apply, the chip text and the chip's power — and the chip's power silently won, so a wrong figure in the apply could not be observed at all. The negative control that broke the apply therefore passed. Repaired with a local: one place decides the cut, and the control now trips.

Live autoplay clean across five real battles, 0 SCRIPT ERROR, with all six firing in ordinary fights — e.g. "Berserker: Blood Offering — spends 35 health for 40 Rage (now 140 HP)", "Gut Rip — Orc Archer bleeds out on 50 buildup" followed by the real bleedout and Slaughterhouse re-seeding the wound, "Precision Strike from the Aggressive guard — two cuts at 20% and +25% parry for 4 turns" then "the guard changes — he comes up Defensive", and "Orc Archer swings at the opening and finds Orc Chief instead (Feint)".

Four suites re-pointed in place, with the reason in each file. The master.html stamp gate is duplicated FOUR times — test_batch_ah, test_batch_bb, test_batch_bn and a fourth copy in test_batch_bo — and all four must move together or a batch that bumps the timestamp trips suites it never touched. Three of test_batch_bo's re-points are inversions: "eighteen ship" became twenty-four, "a Warrior's pool is empty" became "a Warrior drafts two of its own", and "a Warrior's offer is empty" became "a Warrior gets a real offer and a real owed pick". The setups are byte-identical, because they are still what tells the two answers apart. test_batch_bo reads 492/0 (was 449; the count rose because its pool loops walk every entry and six arrived).

Zero drift everywhere else — ah 5500, ah_battle 65, ai 2217, aj 418, ak 528, al 560, ar 914, as 396, at 470, au 336, av 324, aw 350, ax 338, ay 484, az 519, ba 689, bb 172, bc 91, bd 69, be 34, bf 78, bg 47, bh 233, bi 88, bj 67, bm 1890, bn 77, runes 2973, rune_battle 96, all 0 failures. an reads 3618 and bk 129, both inside their documented run-to-run drift (an 3614–3624; bk ±1 by construction) — neither is pinned and neither should be. Two known flakes each reproduced once in the battery and passed on a clean re-run: test_batch_at's Arcane Cannon check (recorded since AV) and test_rune_battle's rune_resist_pierce check (diagnosed in BC — the forced hit still rolls the 5% miss). Neither is on this batch's path.

The designer had no run in flight and none was created; profile.json, relics.json and trees.json are all byte-identical after the battery.

2026-08-13 — Batch BO: the ability draft, and tranche 1

Two things, and the order mattered: §1–§4 build the draft machinery, §5 authors the first tranche of content into it. Machinery first, because the schema decides what an ability has to carry — a draftable flag, a class-wide tag — and authoring ninety-six abilities before those exist means authoring them twice. No save version moves (still v10); no talent node, no magnitude and no existing ability changed.

The rules of the draft

Beside the zone-boss pick there is now a second, larger source of abilities. Where a boss pick is rare and drawn from a small spec pool, the draft is frequent, drawn from its own pool, and comes with a cost.

  • A kit holds seven abilities. Every spec keeps a protected core — its passive, its basic attack, and the minimum abilities its passive needs to function — and that core counts against the seven, so most specs carry 3 protected and 4 draftable (Holy carries 4 and 3).
  • Protected abilities can never be dropped, so every drop decision is among earned abilities. That is what makes the cap unable to break a passive: a Pyromancer can never trade away the Detonation Overburn exists to spend, and a Beastmaster can never trade away the summons Pack Bond reads.
  • At the cap, an offer becomes take-one-and-drop-one. Pick the incoming ability, then name which of your earned abilities it replaces.
  • Declining is always allowed, at any point, full or not. A settled kit is a legitimate end state, and being forced to churn would make a good build worse as the run goes on.
  • A declined or dropped ability does not return to that run's pool, so an offer can never re-present what was just refused.
  • Every offer is 3 cards, and a pool that cannot fill three fills short rather than padding with repeats — the same rule ability upgrades already follow.

Where picks come from. An elite always offers one on victory, to one hero — the designer's decision, and the reason is that the player should always feel their build is improving. Elites run 0–3 per zone, so drafts are route-dependent: a greedy route drafts far more than a cautious one, which is the map's risk axis paying out in a new currency. A merchant sells one for gold (120 / 180 / 240 by zone). Some events trade for one — the Ancient Training Grounds sells a new form for 200 gold, and the Echo of the Warden teaches one for a hero's health. Zone bosses keep the existing pick, unchanged: same timing, same pool, same overlay.

Roughly one card in four is class-wide; the rest are spec. Class-wide abilities are deliberately untied and general — Magic Barrier, not Frostbolt — and are weaker than spec abilities but unconditional: they feed no passive, so at equal power they would be a safe default that dilutes every build. Slightly weaker and always-on makes them the pick you take when your spec's engine is not online yet. Six per class, twenty-four in all, and none of them ship in this batch; the pools are named so the machinery has the shape it needs.

Pools are thin, and that is expected. Eighteen abilities exist against a target of about ninety-six, so offers will come up two cards or one for the next two batches, and a Warrior has no draft pool at all yet. That is the pool filling, not the draft misbehaving.

The twelve protected cores

The "needs" column is the minimum each passive cannot function without, and it is decided per spec rather than by a rule — Overburn is meaningless without a Burn applier and without Detonation to spend it, so the Pyromancer's core is larger than the Berserker's, whose Blood Frenzy reads nothing but his own health bar. An empty cell is a real answer: those specs' opening abilities are protected because they shipped in the kit, not because the passive needs them. The table is written down and tested because the failure it prevents is silent — a spine that stops working because its enabler became draftable logs nothing and simply reads as a weak spec.

SpecSlotsIts passive needsWhy
Berserker3—Blood Frenzy reads his own health bar and nothing else.
Warden3—Heavy Plating is a Block-chance rule; it reads no ability.
Swordmaster3Guard ChangeSeasoned Fighter is two stances, and Guard Change is the only stance swap in the game.
Pyromancer3Fireball, DetonationOverburn needs an applier to build the field and a spender to empty it.
Cryomancer3Frostbolt, Ice LanceGlacial Hold needs a Chilled applier to reach four stacks and a release to end the hold.
Arcanist3Arcane ExplosionRunaway Resonance builds on damaging casts; the free core attack guarantees one every turn.
Holy4Heal, Hymn of HopeMercy must be spendable — Hymn pays stacks outright, and Empower needs a heal to empower.
Devout3Divine Shield, Consecrated GroundConviction builds only on Divine Shield absorbs, and Batch BI measured the ground's drip at 66% of all Faith.
Occultist3Shadowrend, Hex of RuinWrath of the Old Gods marks on debuffs he applies; these two are the debuffs he always holds.
Beastmaster3All three summonsPack Bond reads a living beast. His five opening abilities cost three slots — the summon picker has been one bar entry since Batch AH, and counting three would cost him two draftable picks for a bookkeeping reason.
Sharpshooter3Quick ShotLethal Aim counts consecutive single-target attacks; the free shot lets him stay on a mark every turn.
Survivalist3—Trapper's poison rides being struck, and its breadth term counts statuses from any source — including his allies'.

Tranche 1 — eighteen abilities

Eighteen ship, not twenty-four, and that is said plainly rather than left as a gap: six MAGE, six CLERIC, six HUNTER. The six WARRIOR abilities are not authored here — the Warrior lane names only arrived in Batch BM and their pools want the same discussion the other nine had. Every ability names the axis it serves, because the twelve tree batches spent themselves removing nodes that existed to fill a grid, and ninety-six abilities has the same risk in a larger form.

Pyromancer — different answers to how do you commit.

  • Cinderfall — 30 Mana, 3.0 initiative, 3cd, all enemies. 20% of Attack across the field, then consumes 2 turns of Burn from each enemy to deal that much again to it (Perfect: 3 turns). Overburn refunds every turn taken. Axis: spending wide instead of deep — Detonation empties one bank, this skims every bank, and relieves Overburn's drain across the field.
  • Ember Debt — 20 Mana, 2.0, 4cd, one enemy. No damage. Applies 8 turns of Burn (Perfect: 12), and Overburn's Mana drain ignores that enemy entirely while its turns still feed the damage bonus. Axis: commitment without the bill — one enemy is free to light; everything else still costs, so the choice is which enemy earns the exemption.

Cryomancer — what do you do with the time you bought.

  • Winter's Toll — 25 Mana, 2.5, 4cd. The held enemy takes 8% of Attack per turn it has been held (Perfect: 12%). The hold continues. Axis: cashing in without releasing — Shatter and Ice Lance both end the hold to be paid; this collects interest and leaves the prison standing.
  • Rimebinding — 20 Mana, 2.0, 3cd, one enemy. Applies Chilled equal to the stacks on the held enemy (Perfect: one more). Axis: the hold as a template — one deep prison becomes the next one.

Arcanist — what carries you through the early game.

  • Null Field — 25 Mana, 2.0, 4cd, self. For 3 turns (Perfect: 4), damage taken falls 5% per Resonance stack, read live. Axis: the ramp defends itself — worthless at 2 stacks, enormous at 12, so it does not rescue his early game, it makes surviving to the late game the reward for ramping. It is deliberately the opposite of Stabilize, which Batch AT vaulted for being an escape hatch.
  • Kindled Mind — 15 Mana, 1.5, 2cd, one enemy. 15% of Attack, and it banks 3 Resonance instead of 1 (Perfect: 4). Axis: buying the ramp with tempo — his fastest early climb and a card he would rather not cast late, which is the correct shape for escalation.

Holy — what does reversal cost you.

  • Second Wind — 30 Mana, 2.5, 4cd, one ally. Heals for the damage that ally has taken in the last two turns, up to 40% of their maximum (Perfect: 55%). Axis: undoing recent history rather than topping up — enormous on someone just spiked, worthless on someone healthy.
  • Rite of Return — 35 Mana, 3.0, 5cd, one ally. For 3 turns (Perfect: 4), the next blow that would fell that ally restores them to 50% health instead, and Holy loses 30% of hers. Axis: reversal bought in advance rather than reacted to — Resurrection undoes a death and costs Mercy; this prevents one and costs her health. It sits alongside Intercession, which also refuses a lethal blow: the two are distinguished by target (one named ally vs the next hero to fall), timing (pre-bought vs a window) and currency (her health vs a Mercy stack). If they read as duplicates in play, this is the one to cut.

Devout — what else can he lend.

  • Vow of Suffering — 20 Mana, 2.0, 3cd, one ally. For 3 turns (Perfect: 4), half the damage that ally takes is redirected to the Devout. Axis: mitigation by relocation — Divine Shield absorbs a capped amount; this has no cap, and every hit he eats builds that ally's Faith.
  • Aegis Reversal — 30 Mana, 2.5, 4cd, one ally. Consumes that ally's Divine Shield; their next attack deals bonus damage equal to what the shield had left (Perfect: half again). Axis: unspent protection becomes offence — his shields expire unspent constantly; this makes over-shielding a resource, and it is the Devout dealing damage without attacking.

Occultist — what else can be corrupted.

  • Blight the Well — 25 Mana, 2.5, 4cd, one enemy. For 4 turns (Perfect: 6), healing that enemy receives damages it instead. Axis: corrupting recovery. Deliberately situational — near-dead against a warband with no healer, decisive against one with. A pool needs a few cards that are excellent when the board calls for them, or every pick is a safe generic. It is a real debuff, so a mender can spend a turn cleansing it, and that is the counterplay rather than an oversight.
  • Covenant of Ash — 20 Mana, 2.0, 4cd, one enemy. Marks it; while the mark holds, every Ruin stack applied to any enemy also lands here. One covenant at a time. Axis: corruption that compounds onto a chosen target — spread pressure becomes focused pressure without giving up the spread.

Beastmaster — what does the partnership give you.

  • Twin Hunt — 25 Mana, 2.5, 3cd, one enemy. The Beastmaster and his beast strike together, each for 40% of Attack (Perfect: the beast lands 55%). If the beast's blow kills it, his next ability costs nothing. Axis: the two bodies acting deliberately, where the rest of his kit has the beast on its own clock.
  • Call the Wilds — 20 Mana, 2.0, 5cd. Calls in the absent beast he is most bonded with, keeping its Loyalty and paying no shared Swap cooldown, and it strikes immediately (Perfect: twice). Axis: rotation without the tax. Batch BJ measured swaps at 0.05 per trash battle — the central verb of an entire lane barely happens. A CORRECTION TRAVELS WITH THIS ONE. The batch brief says the Loyalty loss is why, and a swap has never cost Loyalty: it lives on the hunter's own record and is written in four places, none of them a swap — only a beast's death breaks the meter, and only Primal Surge spends it. What a swap actually costs is a turn and a three-turn shared cooldown, so that is what this ability refuses. The Loyalty half is kept as an explicit, tested guarantee rather than dropped, because it is what the card promises.

Sharpshooter — what breaks the patience, and what pays for it.

  • Called Volley — 30 Mana, 3.0, 4cd, all enemies. 20% of Attack (Perfect: 26%), and his Focus and his mark survive it. Axis: hitting the field without breaking the bond. A finding travels with this one. The clause is true but it is not a distinction: the Focus engine has been gated to skip area attacks since Batch AZ, so no area attack has ever cleared Focus, and "every other multi-target option costs him his meter" does not survive reading the code either. Shipped as specified; what the batch buys is that the guarantee is now a named, tested rule rather than a side effect of one condition in an unrelated branch.
  • Quarry's Mark — 15 Mana, 1.5, 3cd, one enemy. Marks it for the battle: Focus gained from attacking the marked enemy is doubled. One mark at a time. Axis: patience that accelerates. It deliberately does not protect Focus on the marked enemy's death — Overkill already does that, and an ability duplicating a row-7 talent is the Deadfall fault Batch BD found. Switching still clears him; the mark makes committing pay faster. Flag: doubled may be too much on a build stacking Muscle Memory and Unwavering. +50% is the stated fallback if it reads hot in play.

Survivalist — what else can be worn down.

  • Choking Smoke — 25 Mana, 2.5, 4cd, all enemies. Blinds every enemy for 2 turns (Perfect: 3). Axis: an affliction his kit does not otherwise have. It uses the existing Blind at its existing +50% miss rather than authoring a new status; the stacking was read rather than assumed, and it is flat percentage points on top of the base 5%, never a multiplier. Priced against the fact that area attacks never miss, so a field-wide 50% blanks single-target blows only.
  • Snare Line — 25 Mana, 2.5, 4cd. Every enemy that acts next turn springs one of your traps, wherever it stands (Perfect: the line holds two turns). Axis: the traps stop waiting. It fills no trap slot and spends no placed trap, and the spring goes through the same door a snare does — so Bone Breaker's Break, Cruel Devices' multiplier, Quick Rigging's Cripple and Caught Fast all pay per spring.

Reported, not buried

  • Break damage is this batch's number, not the brief's. The batch specifies cost, initiative, cooldown, target and effect for all eighteen and says nothing about Break damage — exactly as Batch AT's brief said nothing about Death Ray's, and that omission became a thread three batches long. The four that are ordinary attacks carry Break damage in line with their siblings (Cinderfall 8, Called Volley 8, Kindled Mind 6, Twin Hunt 12); the rest are not attacks and carry none.
  • A name collision this batch created. Second Wind is also the Berserker's Warpath row-5 talent node. It is a label collision only — a node's name is not an ability name, nothing resolves it, and the two write different fields — so nothing breaks. Shipped as specified and flagged, on the Shared Vigil and Overkill precedents: renaming either is the designer's call and one string.
  • Two decisions the brief did not make. An elite's draft goes to one hero, drawn at random and independently of the rune looter: the brief says "always, on victory" and not to whom, and offering every hero a card at every elite would hand out about twenty-six picks a run against four draftable slots, which is not a draft. And the merchant's price (120/180/240) sits deliberately below the blacksmith's, because the smith buys a permanent upgrade to something you already hold while an ability is the cheaper, more frequent question.
  • The cap binds the zone-boss pick too. The offer is unchanged — same source, same timing, same pool — but a seven-slot cap one pool can walk past is not a cap, and a Beastmaster's five spec-pool entries alone reach eight. A boss pick at the cap opens the same drop step a drafted card does.
  • The bot gets one hook, not nine rotations. Every spec's rotation is hand-authored and names its own kit, so a drafted ability would never be cast and no sim or live autoplay could verify it. The bot now substitutes a usable drafted ability only when its real rotation came back with the free basic attack — so it re-weights nothing, and no measurement taken before this batch stops being comparable. It is not a policy for playing these abilities well.

Verification

Per the designer's standing instruction from this batch onward, verification means the code landed and works. No sweeps, no bands, no balance measurement was run, and none should be quoted from this batch. Where a number above needs watching, it is watched in play.

Parse and flow gates clean, thirteen scenes with no script errors, the map-screen draw gate green, the run-harness gates all passing, and a new test_batch_bo.gd at 449 checks, 0 failures, stable over four runs. Eight negative controls were each applied to real code and reverted (every touched file came back byte-identical by hash): a protected ability made droppable trips seven checks; Ember Debt's two denominators collapsed into one trips one; Null Field frozen at cast time trips one; a decline that does not write the no-return ledger trips three; an offer padding with repeats trips three; Winter's Toll releasing the hold trips two; an enabler moved into its own draft pool trips two; and Rite of Return billing its toll before restoring the health trips two. All eight of the clauses that could have silently done nothing — Ember Debt's drain exemption, Winter's Toll leaving the hold standing, Null Field reading current stacks, Aegis Reversal consuming the shield, Covenant of Ash mirroring Ruin, Call the Wilds preserving Loyalty, Called Volley preserving Focus, and Choking Smoke applying the existing Blind — are driven live and asserted against the state they change, never against the fact that the cast returned. Three of them are built so that a broken implementation still fails: Winter's Toll's "the hold continues" is trivially true of an ability that does nothing, so the damage is asserted beside it; Null Field's "reads current stacks" is trivially true of a value stamped at cast time, so the same field is measured at two different stack counts; and Call the Wilds' "keeps Loyalty" is trivially true if no swap happened, so the beast on the field is asserted to have changed.

One edge was found by reading rather than by a test, and it is worth recording: Holy can swear the Rite of Return on herself. Her own 30% toll then re-enters the very reversal she is standing in — so the promise has to be spent before the bill (or it answers its own toll forever) and the health restored before it (or she is billed against zero and marked dead one line before the restore would have run). That ordering is why this reversal's handler performs the restore while Intercession's and Martyrdom's do not, and the self-save case is now driven directly.

Live autoplay is clean across three real battles, with six of the eighteen firing in ordinary fights. A six-run simulation was walked only to exercise the elite path — eight offers a run, seven and a half cards shown, four and two thirds taken, and the short-offer and nothing-left-to-offer counters both large, which is exactly the shape of a thin pool. Its difficulty figures are not a reading and none is quoted.

The full suite battery is green with zero drift, and the zero was measured rather than assumed — seven suites were re-run on stashed, unmodified code and read the same counts to the check. Two pre-existing faults are recorded rather than fixed: test_batch_ah and test_batch_an both still call a function Batch BM deleted, and each throws an error that aborts its own section while the suite goes on printing "0 failures" — so both have been quietly under-testing their reward sections for two batches. And test_batch_al's long-standing Spite flake appeared once in four runs, as it has since Batch BI. Neither is on this batch's path.

2026-08-13 — Batch BN: the crash, and the gate

Two items, both blockers, neither large. §1 is a bug. §2 is one float, chosen by measurement rather than by guess. Nothing else ships: no node, no ability, no magnitude and no save version moved. The capstone repricing that BM's numbers argue for is the next batch's — it wants the leave-one-out grid owed on Harmonic Convergence, and it must not be tuned in the same pass that moves rung 1.

§1 — The infinite recursion between a release and a freeze

A Cryomancer who had taken Honed Shards could crash the game in normal play, and had been able to for six batches. Batch BF's built sweep found it (23 stack overflows per 400 budget-12 battles, identical on unmodified code, so it was nobody's recent work); Batch BG then measured 15 overflows in four runs and about a megabyte of backtrace with a Thaw Cryomancer in the party, and had to substitute a different lane to get a run measurement at all.

The mechanism is deterministic, and neither half of it was a bug on its own. A released enemy comes back on 1 stack of Chilled and Honed Shards then applies 3 — which is exactly the 4 that flash-freezes — so every release re-froze its own target. The code comment beside that node had always said so, and it was right. The half nobody had read next to it is that a Thaw Cryomancer holds one enemy, so freezing a second evicts the first, and an eviction is a release. Together: freeze B, evict A, A's release re-freezes A, which evicts B, whose release re-freezes B — until the engine's stack limit.

  • The fix is a re-entrancy guard, not a magnitude change. No freeze may begin while a release is resolving. Every authored number is left exactly as it was — the node's intent is that a released enemy goes back into the pile deep, the engine stays warm, and that intent is good. Cutting Honed Shards' 3 or the release's 1 to dodge a threshold would have nerfed a node to work around control flow.
  • It also closes the milder version that was never a crash: Ice Lance releasing a hold and instantly re-taking it, which made the release read as doing nothing.
  • A parked enemy is still freezable, which was verified rather than assumed — the freeze threshold reads being at four stacks rather than reaching four, so the very next chill freezes it again. A guard that left enemies permanently unfreezable would have been a worse bug than the crash, and a silent one.
  • Cryoclasm is unaffected. It moves a hold rather than spending one and is deliberately not routed through the release, so no release payoff fires and the guard never sees it.

What it unblocks, measured: a Thaw Cryomancer can be measured by the run harness again — 25 runs, zero stack-overflow events, 16% completions at rung 2. The matched control on unmodified code could not finish: 305 overflow events and 20 MB of backtrace by its sixth run, in the time the fixed build completed all twenty-five.

§2 — Rung 1's multiplier: ×0.70 → ×0.50

Difficulty 1 is the gate the entire meta layer sits behind — rows, points, capstones and every subsequent rung are all behind one end-boss kill at rung 1, played with no talents at all. It was completing 12–13%, which is roughly eight attempts before the first unlock with nothing banked in between. Rung 1 is the tutorial rung and it has to behave like one.

The number was swept, not guessed. Untalented party, balanced route, n=100 a row:

Rung-1 multiplierCompletionsDepth (of 49)Power ratio @ z1 t8Median wipe
×0.70 (was)13%33.33 ±1.191.25slot 30 (z2 t14)
×0.6028%39.68 ±0.951.45slot 41 (z3 t9)
×0.50 (ships)83%46.62 ±0.431.74slot 42 (z3 t10)
×0.4095%47.76 ±0.172.15slot 45 (z3 t13)

The aim is a first run won in one or two attempts — 70% is 1.4 attempts, and 83% is 1.2. ×0.50 is the closest of the four and it ships, but the table is what matters: the curve is a cliff between ×0.60 and ×0.50 (55 points across one step), so 70% sits inside an unsampled gap and this is the nearest sampled value rather than a number defended to three figures. A later playtest may move it. The ×0.70 row reproduces Batch BM's 12% at half the sample size, which is the control that makes the rest readable.

Three consequences, reported rather than discovered

  • Rung 1 with talent rows 1–3 filled is now close to a formality — that build read 52% under ×0.70 and 96% now (n=100, balanced). This is correct and not a problem. Rung 1 is meant to become trivial once a player has anything, because that is the signal to climb.
  • Rungs 2 and 3 did not move. Neither multiplier, neither twist. Rung 2 is the present balance byte for byte, which is what keeps every earlier measurement readable. This is stated because a batch that moved rung 1 is exactly where someone would later assume all three moved.
  • The 0% at rung 2 is not addressed here and must not be papered over by moving a rung. Batch BM measured rung-matched builds at 52% / 0% / 18% and diagnosed it correctly: the ladder's power ratio is a clean, monotone 1.26 / 0.91 / 0.67, so the ladder is fine and the tree is uneven — a capstone is worth more than the eight lane nodes beneath it put together. That is a distribution problem and it belongs to its own batch.

2026-08-12 — Batch BM: talents become meta progression

The largest design change in the project's history. Talents stop being an in-run reward and become a permanent, vertical meta layer: points are earned per spec per completed zone, banked forever, and spent between runs. Rows unlock by beating the end boss at escalating difficulty. A fresh save has no rows and no points. NO BALANCE WAS TUNED IN THIS BATCH. Every figure in §7 below is a NEW BASELINE, and the batch that creates a baseline must not correct against it — that is how four consecutive Devout batches went wrong. Difficulty and magnitude tuning are the designer's, recorded here so they are not rediscovered as an omission.

§4 — The structure, first, because everything else is downstream of it

BUYING A CELL UNLOCKS AN OPTION. IT DOES NOT EQUIP IT. This is the load-bearing distinction in the whole design and it is the thing a later batch would most easily collapse. A cell is bought once, permanently, out of that spec's banked points. Equipping is a separate act: you still pick ONE node per row, and it locks for the run. Owning all three cells in a row means that row is a real three-way argument; owning one means it has no argument in it. That is why twenty batches of row pricing describe the endgame rather than needing a third pass — a row is still priced against two closed doors.

  • Earning: 1 point per spec per zone boss defeated. Three zone bosses, so 3 per spec per completed run, and a run that dies in zone 2 still banks 1 or 2 — that partial credit is the mechanism, not a separate rule. Only specs that were in the party earn. Points are per spec and never transfer. Banking is uncapped.
  • Spending: cells cost by tier — rows 1–3 cost 1, rows 4–6 cost 2, rows 7–9 cost 3. 27 cells = 54 points to fill one spec, which at 3 a run is eighteen completions.
  • Respec: free, any time outside a run, never during one. Nothing is spent per run; the 54 points are a permanent unlock ledger, not a per-run budget.
  • Row gating is GLOBAL — rows 1–3 at difficulty 1, 4–6 at 2, 7–9 at 3, for every spec at once. Points are per spec; rows are not. A tier arrives fully unlocked, which is what an uncapped bank is for.
  • The ledger lives on Profile, which already survived outside the run save. Profile is v2, with the tolerant load it always had: an existing profile arrives with zero points and zero rows, the correct state for a save that has never completed a run under this system. The run save is v10 and a pre-v10 save is REFUSED and cleared — the final zone gained a seventeenth slot, so a v9 map has no position to walk onto after its boss, and inventing one is worse than a wipe that says so.
  • The build screen (scenes/talents.tscn) is reachable from the main menu and from the draft. Per spec: points available, the row grid with locked / owned / equipped states, tier costs, and a free respec. In-run the tree is read-only and the hero sheet says so in its header.

A discrepancy in the brief, flagged rather than resolved silently: the designer said a first completion grants 5 points per spec; the mechanism pays 3. Three ships, because it is what the mechanism produces and because rows 1–3 at tier-1 prices cost exactly 3 — the first completion buys a complete minimal build, one node per row. If a first-completion bonus was intended, it is one number and it is not in this batch.

§2 — Thirty-six new row-8 nodes, with the rationale for each

Every tree is 9 rows now: 1–8 are lane rows, 9 is the capstone shelf. Row 8 is not "row 7 but bigger" — it is the node that only matters once the rest of the lane is bought. Each one reads an accumulated quantity the lane has spent seven rows building, removes a constraint the lane has worked around, or converts the lane's currency into something it could not previously buy. None is a larger magnitude of a node above it, and that is asserted structurally rather than promised: test_batch_bm walks every lane and fails any row-8 payload that writes a stat field an earlier node in the same lane writes.

Spec / laneNodeWhy it is a row 8
